Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I’m here because things have just kind of gone sideways since they made me the acting workflow lead at the warehouse. I mean, I’ve always had stressful days there — it’s shipping and receiving, inventory, SAP, cycle counts, all that stuff — but lately I’m walking in already tense, like before anything has even happened.

And then small things that I used to just fix and move on from, like little SAP errors or someone entering something wrong, I’m snapping over them. Not screaming or anything, but I can hear myself getting short with people, and that’s not really how I like to be. I’m also missing cycle-count deadlines that I honestly never used to miss, which makes me feel worse because that was one of the things I was dependable about.

By the time I get home, I’m just… drained. I don’t even want to play basketball, which is usually how I blow off steam, and I haven’t really been working on my sketches either. I guess I came in because it feels like this role change flipped some switch in me, and I’m not handling it the way I thought I would.

Emotionally, I feel like I’m kind of failing at something I thought I should be able to handle. Since they made me the acting workflow lead, I keep walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m braced for something to go wrong before the day even starts. And then when something small does happen — like a little SAP error, or a count issue, or somebody missing a detail — I react more sharply than I want to.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the person who could just deal with that stuff. Cycle counts, shipping issues, inventory fixes — I wasn’t perfect, but I was dependable. Now I’m missing deadlines I never used to miss, and then I start thinking, “What’s wrong with me? Why can’t I keep up?” So it kind of feeds on itself.

By the time I get home, I’m just drained. I don’t even feel like playing basketball, which is usually how I reset, and I haven’t been working on my sketches much either. It’s like work takes up all my energy, and then I’m left feeling frustrated with myself because I’m not handling the role the way I thought I would.

Yeah… definitely. Basketball used to be the thing I’d make time for no matter what, even if I was tired. Like, I’d go shoot around or play with the guys and it would kind of clear my head. But lately I just don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’m too wiped out from work, or I need to catch up on some warehouse thing, or I just want to sit there and not think.

And sketching too — that was usually my quiet time. I’d sit down for an hour or two and work on whatever little ideas I had, and now I barely touch it. It’s not even that I decided to quit or anything, it’s more like work has taken over all the space in my head, and by the time I get home there’s nothing left for anything else.

It’s gotten to where it’s affecting work and home at the same time. My supervisor actually had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt awful, because I never used to be the person people had to remind about that stuff. So then I go home feeling embarrassed and behind, and instead of doing basketball or sketching like I normally would, I just… don’t.

Why can’t I keep up?” So it kind of feeds on itself.\n\nBy the time I get home, I’m just drained. I keep telling myself I’m too wiped out from work, or I need to catch up on some warehouse thing, or I just want to sit there and not think.\n\nAnd sketching too — that was usually my quiet time. My supervisor actually had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt awful, because I never used to be the person people had to remind about that stuff. I don’t know if I’d say it’s down most of the day, nearly every day. It doesn’t feel like I wake up sad and stay sad straight through, if that makes sense.

It’s more like… work is the thing that sets it off, or even thinking about work. Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. But I’m not sure I’d describe my mood as low all day regardless of what’s happening. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.

I guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me how you’ve been feeling emotionally lately, especially about yourself and your ability to handle things at work?\n\nPatient: Emotionally, I feel like I’m kind of failing at something I thought I should be able to handle. Since they made me the acting workflow lead, I keep walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m braced for something to go wrong before the day even starts. And then when something small does happen — like a little SAP error, or a count issue, or somebody missing a detail — I react more sharply than I want to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the person who could just deal with that stuff. Why can’t I keep up?” So it kind of feeds on itself.\n\nBy the time I get home, I’m just drained. I don’t even feel like playing basketball, which is usually how I reset, and I haven’t been working on my sketches much either. It’s like work takes up all my energy, and then I’m left feeling frustrated with myself because I’m not handling the role the way I thought I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of activities like basketball or sketching as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Basketball used to be the thing I’d make time for no matter what, even if I was tired. Like, I’d go shoot around or play with the guys and it would kind of clear my head. But lately I just don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’m too wiped out from work, or I need to catch up on some warehouse thing, or I just want to sit there and not think.\n\nAnd sketching too — that was usually my quiet time. I’d sit down for an hour or two and work on whatever little ideas I had, and now I barely touch it. It’s not even that I decided to quit or anything, it’s more like work has taken over all the space in my head, and by the time I get home there’s nothing left for anything else.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it’s affecting work and home at the same time. My supervisor actually had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt awful, because I never used to be the person people had to remind about that stuff. So then I go home feeling embarrassed and behind, and instead of doing basketball or sketching like I normally would, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down most of the day, nearly every day, not just during or after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s down most of the day, nearly every day. It doesn’t feel like I wake up sad and stay sad straight through, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… work is the thing that sets it off, or even thinking about work. Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. But I’m not sure I’d describe my mood as low all day regardless of what’s happening. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.\n\nI guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / shipping_receiving_or_inventory_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, snapping over little SAP errors, missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and then going home too drained to play basketball or even work on my sketches.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.

Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.

But if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.

So then I go home feeling embarrassed and behind, and instead of doing basketball or sketching like I normally would, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down most of the day, nearly every day, not just during or after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s down most of the day, nearly every day. It doesn’t feel like I wake up sad and stay sad straight through, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… work is the thing that sets it off, or even thinking about work. Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. But I’m not sure I’d describe my mood as low all day regardless of what’s happening. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.\n\nI guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / shipping_receiving_or_inventory_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.

With other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. Some stuff still feels okay if I actually get myself into it, like if I’m around people or something is already happening. It’s not like every single thing feels empty or pointless. I don’t think I’ve lost interest in everything across the board.

It feels more like I’m tired and preoccupied, and the things that take effort — like getting changed and going to play, or sitting down and starting a drawing — those are the things that have dropped off. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.

I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.\n\nWith other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. Some stuff still feels okay if I actually get myself into it, like if I’m around people or something is already happening. It’s not like every single thing feels empty or pointless. I don’t think I’ve lost interest in everything across the board.\n\nIt feels more like I’m tired and preoccupied, and the things that take effort — like getting changed and going to play, or sitting down and starting a drawing — those are the things that have dropped off. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’ve been feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / shipping_receiving_or_inventory_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, snapping over little SAP errors, missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and then going home too drained to play basketball or even work on my sketches.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… tired is probably one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. I don’t know if it’s “for no reason,” exactly, because it feels connected to the whole warehouse situation, but by the time I get home I’m just wiped out in a way that seems bigger than the work itself.

Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead spot, I feel like I’m walking into the warehouse already braced for something to go wrong. I’m tense before the day even starts, and then I’m dealing with SAP mistakes or people missing something small, and I’ll snap over stuff I probably would’ve handled better before. Then I’m behind on cycle counts or worrying about deadlines I used to stay on top of, and it just drains me.

So even if I’m not doing anything physically demanding after work, I get home and I don’t have anything left. Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.

Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, even things you used to enjoy outside of work, not just basketball or sketching?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’d say absolutely everything. I know I probably keep coming back to basketball and sketching because those are the easiest examples for me to explain, but it’s not like the whole world has gone flat or nothing can feel good at all.

It’s more like… the stuff that used to be easy to reach for, I just don’t reach for it as much. If something takes initiative, like deciding to go play or sitting down and starting a drawing, that’s where I’ve been getting stuck. But I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in every single thing outside of work.

I mean, if I’m already in the middle of something, or someone else kind of pulls me into it, I can still enjoy parts of it. So “absolutely everything” feels too strong. It’s not that nothing matters or nothing feels good. It’s more that I’m worn down and kind of preoccupied, and that makes the things I used to enjoy feel harder to start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’m completely incapable of enjoying anything if work isn’t on my mind, but it has gotten harder. That’s probably why I’m here, honestly. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.

I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, so that piles on too. By the time I get home, even if I’m technically not thinking about work right that second, I’m just… drained.

So with basketball, I’ll think, “I should go play, that would probably help,” but getting myself to actually do it feels like a lot. Same with sketching. I used to just sit down and start messing around with a drawing, and now I’ll have the thought, but I don’t follow through. It’s like the interest is still somewhere in there, but I don’t have the energy to reach it most days.

Not screaming or anything, but I can hear myself getting short with people, and that’s not really how I like to be. Since they made me the acting workflow lead, I keep walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m braced for something to go wrong before the day even starts. And then when something small does happen — like a little SAP error, or a count issue, or somebody missing a detail — I react more sharply than I want to.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the person who could just deal with that stuff. Cycle counts, shipping issues, inventory fixes — I wasn’t perfect, but I was dependable. Now I’m missing deadlines I never used to miss, and then I start thinking, “What’s wrong with me? I don’t even feel like playing basketball, which is usually how I reset, and I haven’t been working on my sketches much either. It’s like work takes up all my energy, and then I’m left feeling frustrated with myself because I’m not handling the role the way I thought I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of activities like basketball or sketching as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. Basketball used to be the thing I’d make time for no matter what, even if I was tired. Like, I’d go shoot around or play with the guys and it would kind of clear my head. But lately I just don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’m too wiped out from work, or I need to catch up on some warehouse thing, or I just want to sit there and not think.\n\nAnd sketching too — that was usually my quiet time. I’d sit down for an hour or two and work on whatever little ideas I had, and now I barely touch it. It’s not even that I decided to quit or anything, it’s more like work has taken over all the space in my head, and by the time I get home there’s nothing left for anything else.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it’s affecting work and home at the same time. My supervisor actually had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt awful, because I never used to be the person people had to remind about that stuff. So then I go home feeling embarrassed and behind, and instead of doing basketball or sketching like I normally would, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down most of the day, nearly every day, not just during or after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s down most of the day, nearly every day. It doesn’t feel like I wake up sad and stay sad straight through, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… work is the thing that sets it off, or even thinking about work. Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. But I’m not sure I’d describe my mood as low all day regardless of what’s happening. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.\n\nI guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… tired is probably one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.\n\nI guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.\n\nWith other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. Some stuff still feels okay if I actually get myself into it, like if I’m around people or something is already happening. It’s not like every single thing feels empty or pointless. I don’t think I’ve lost interest in everything across the board.\n\nIt feels more like I’m tired and preoccupied, and the things that take effort — like getting changed and going to play, or sitting down and starting a drawing — those are the things that have dropped off. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… tired is probably one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. I don’t know if it’s “for no reason,” exactly, because it feels connected to the whole warehouse situation, but by the time I get home I’m just wiped out in a way that seems bigger than the work itself.\n\nEver since they put me in that acting workflow lead spot, I feel like I’m walking into the warehouse already braced for something to go wrong. I’m tense before the day even starts, and then I’m dealing with SAP mistakes or people missing something small, and I’ll snap over stuff I probably would’ve handled better before. Then I’m behind on cycle counts or worrying about deadlines I used to stay on top of, and it just drains me.\n\nSo even if I’m not doing anything physically demanding after work, I get home and I don’t have anything left. Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, even things you used to enjoy outside of work, not just basketball or sketching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say absolutely everything. I know I probably keep coming back to basketball and sketching because those are the easiest examples for me to explain, but it’s not like the whole world has gone flat or nothing can feel good at all.\n\nIt’s more like… the stuff that used to be easy to reach for, I just don’t reach for it as much. If something takes initiative, like deciding to go play or sitting down and starting a drawing, that’s where I’ve been getting stuck. But I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in every single thing outside of work.\n\nI mean, if I’m already in the middle of something, or someone else kind of pulls me into it, I can still enjoy parts of it. So “absolutely everything” feels too strong. It’s not that nothing matters or nothing feels good. It’s more that I’m worn down and kind of preoccupied, and that makes the things I used to enjoy feel harder to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel any interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’m completely incapable of enjoying anything if work isn’t on my mind, but it has gotten harder. That’s probably why I’m here, honestly. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.\n\nI walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, so that piles on too. By the time I get home, even if I’m technically not thinking about work right that second, I’m just… drained.\n\nSo with basketball, I’ll think, “I should go play, that would probably help,” but getting myself to actually do it feels like a lot. Same with sketching. I used to just sit down and start messing around with a drawing, and now I’ll have the thought, but I don’t follow through. It’s like the interest is still somewhere in there, but I don’t have the energy to reach it most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I mean… I think so, but I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I can separate it cleanly in my head. Even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, the warehouse,” or replaying something that happened, it feels like the stress has already kind of taken up space in me.

Then I’m behind on cycle counts or worrying about deadlines I used to stay on top of, and it just drains me.\n\nSo even if I’m not doing anything physically demanding after work, I get home and I don’t have anything left. Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, even things you used to enjoy outside of work, not just basketball or sketching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say absolutely everything. It’s not that I hate them now or that they never sound good, but the interest and pleasure are harder to get to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’ve felt little interest or pleasure in doing things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or the stress from the warehouse?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / shipping_receiving_or_inventory_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, snapping over little SAP errors, missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and then going home too drained to play basketball or even work on my sketches.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I’ve noticed it, though I keep feeling like I need to explain it because it’s not like I just woke up one day and stopped liking things.

It’s more that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’m carrying all this tension around. I’ll get to the warehouse already on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then some little SAP error comes up or someone misses a small detail, and I react way bigger than I should. Then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that just makes me feel like I’m falling behind at something I used to handle fine.

So by the time I get home, even if I’m not sitting there actively thinking about work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be something I’d do without thinking too hard about it, and sketching was the same — I could just sit down and mess around with it. Lately, I’ll think, “I should go play,” or “I should work on a sketch,” but I don’t really get myself there. It feels like the enjoyment is still there somewhere, but I’m too worn out to reach it most days.

Basketball used to be the thing I’d make time for no matter what, even if I was tired. Like, I’d go shoot around or play with the guys and it would kind of clear my head. But lately I just don’t show up. I keep telling myself I’m too wiped out from work, or I need to catch up on some warehouse thing, or I just want to sit there and not think.\n\nAnd sketching too — that was usually my quiet time. I’d sit down for an hour or two and work on whatever little ideas I had, and now I barely touch it. It’s not even that I decided to quit or anything, it’s more like work has taken over all the space in my head, and by the time I get home there’s nothing left for anything else.\n\nIt’s gotten to where it’s affecting work and home at the same time. My supervisor actually had to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt awful, because I never used to be the person people had to remind about that stuff. So then I go home feeling embarrassed and behind, and instead of doing basketball or sketching like I normally would, I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down most of the day, nearly every day, not just during or after work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s down most of the day, nearly every day. It doesn’t feel like I wake up sad and stay sad straight through, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… work is the thing that sets it off, or even thinking about work. Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. But I’m not sure I’d describe my mood as low all day regardless of what’s happening. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.\n\nWith other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. Some stuff still feels okay if I actually get myself into it, like if I’m around people or something is already happening. It’s not like every single thing feels empty or pointless. I don’t think I’ve lost interest in everything across the board.\n\nIt feels more like I’m tired and preoccupied, and the things that take effort — like getting changed and going to play, or sitting down and starting a drawing — those are the things that have dropped off. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… tired is probably one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. I don’t know if it’s “for no reason,” exactly, because it feels connected to the whole warehouse situation, but by the time I get home I’m just wiped out in a way that seems bigger than the work itself.\n\nEver since they put me in that acting workflow lead spot, I feel like I’m walking into the warehouse already braced for something to go wrong. I’m tense before the day even starts, and then I’m dealing with SAP mistakes or people missing something small, and I’ll snap over stuff I probably would’ve handled better before. Then I’m behind on cycle counts or worrying about deadlines I used to stay on top of, and it just drains me.\n\nSo even if I’m not doing anything physically demanding after work, I get home and I don’t have anything left. Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, even things you used to enjoy outside of work, not just basketball or sketching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say absolutely everything. I know I probably keep coming back to basketball and sketching because those are the easiest examples for me to explain, but it’s not like the whole world has gone flat or nothing can feel good at all.\n\nIt’s more like… the stuff that used to be easy to reach for, I just don’t reach for it as much. If something takes initiative, like deciding to go play or sitting down and starting a drawing, that’s where I’ve been getting stuck. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.\n\nI walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. It’s been about four months now, I’d say. It started around when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead role.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always separate it out that well in the moment.

I don’t think I have many days where everything at work is fine and I’m not thinking about it at all, and then I’m just suddenly hopeless for no reason. It’s more like… even on quieter days, there’s still this leftover heaviness or worry in the background, if that makes sense. So I might feel down, but I usually connect it somehow to how things have been going overall, or to feeling worn out from it.

If you mean, like, completely independent of work — where I’m having a good day there, not stressed about it, and still feel hopeless anyway — I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing. It’s hard to say, but it feels tied to everything that’s been happening more than just coming out of nowhere.

Like in the morning before I go in, I’ll start feeling tense and kind of discouraged, and then if the day goes badly it follows me home. It’s not like there’s this constant dark cloud every single hour.\n\nI guess I feel frustrated, embarrassed, and worn out a lot, especially around the warehouse stuff and the lead role. But “down most of the day nearly every day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed. I’d say it comes in waves, mostly tied to work and how behind or on edge I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge even outside of work situations, like at home or during times when you're not thinking about the warehouse or your role there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly anxious all the time for no reason, like sitting at home completely disconnected from work and still panicking. It’s more that work kind of follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I’m carrying the warehouse around in my head even when I’m not physically there. I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.\n\nWith other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. Some stuff still feels okay if I actually get myself into it, like if I’m around people or something is already happening. It’s not like every single thing feels empty or pointless. I don’t think I’ve lost interest in everything across the board.\n\nIt feels more like I’m tired and preoccupied, and the things that take effort — like getting changed and going to play, or sitting down and starting a drawing — those are the things that have dropped off. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been feeling unusually tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… tired is probably one of the main things I’ve noticed, honestly. I don’t know if it’s “for no reason,” exactly, because it feels connected to the whole warehouse situation, but by the time I get home I’m just wiped out in a way that seems bigger than the work itself.\n\nEver since they put me in that acting workflow lead spot, I feel like I’m walking into the warehouse already braced for something to go wrong. I’m tense before the day even starts, and then I’m dealing with SAP mistakes or people missing something small, and I’ll snap over stuff I probably would’ve handled better before. Then I’m behind on cycle counts or worrying about deadlines I used to stay on top of, and it just drains me.\n\nSo even if I’m not doing anything physically demanding after work, I get home and I don’t have anything left. Like basketball used to help me blow off steam, and sketching used to be something I’d sit down and do without thinking too much, but lately I’m too drained to even start. It’s not always that my body is exhausted like I ran all day — it’s more like I’m mentally wrung out, and then I just don’t have the energy for normal things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in absolutely everything, even things you used to enjoy outside of work, not just basketball or sketching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say absolutely everything. I know I probably keep coming back to basketball and sketching because those are the easiest examples for me to explain, but it’s not like the whole world has gone flat or nothing can feel good at all.\n\nIt’s more like… the stuff that used to be easy to reach for, I just don’t reach for it as much. If something takes initiative, like deciding to go play or sitting down and starting a drawing, that’s where I’ve been getting stuck. But I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in every single thing outside of work.\n\nI mean, if I’m already in the middle of something, or someone else kind of pulls me into it, I can still enjoy parts of it. So “absolutely everything” feels too strong. It’s not that nothing matters or nothing feels good. It’s more that I’m worn down and kind of preoccupied, and that makes the things I used to enjoy feel harder to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel any interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’m completely incapable of enjoying anything if work isn’t on my mind, but it has gotten harder. That’s probably why I’m here, honestly. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.\n\nI walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, so that piles on too. By the time I get home, even if I’m technically not thinking about work right that second, I’m just… drained.\n\nSo with basketball, I’ll think, “I should go play, that would probably help,” but getting myself to actually do it feels like a lot. Same with sketching. I used to just sit down and start messing around with a drawing, and now I’ll have the thought, but I don’t follow through. It’s like the interest is still somewhere in there, but I don’t have the energy to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to feel any interest or pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, even when your mind is completely off work and the stress of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think so, but I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I can separate it cleanly in my head. Even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, the warehouse,” or replaying something that happened, it feels like the stress has already kind of taken up space in me.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing before anything even happens. Then it’ll be some small SAP error or someone missing a detail, and I’ll snap more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, which just makes me feel worse about the whole thing.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if my mind is technically off work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be automatic for me — like, I’d go play and it would clear my head. And sketching was the same kind of thing, something I could just sit down and do. Lately I’ll think about doing those things, but I don’t really feel that same pull, or I feel too worn out to start. It’s not that I hate them now or that they never sound good, but the interest and pleasure are harder to get to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve felt little interest or pleasure in doing things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or the stress from the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve noticed it, though I keep feeling like I need to explain it because it’s not like I just woke up one day and stopped liking things.\n\nIt’s more that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’m carrying all this tension around. I’ll get to the warehouse already on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then some little SAP error comes up or someone misses a small detail, and I react way bigger than I should. Then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that just makes me feel like I’m falling behind at something I used to handle fine.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if I’m not sitting there actively thinking about work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be something I’d do without thinking too hard about it, and sketching was the same — I could just sit down and mess around with it. Lately, I’ll think, “I should go play,” or “I should work on a sketch,” but I don’t really get myself there. It feels like the enjoyment is still there somewhere, but I’m too worn out to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the tension, irritability at work, losing interest in basketball and sketching, and feeling drained — from the time these symptoms started until now?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now, I’d say. It started around when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead role.\n\nI don’t remember the exact day where I was like, “Okay, this is different,” but that’s the turning point in my mind. Before that, work had stressful days, sure, but it didn’t follow me around the same way. Since that change, it’s just been this steady thing — the tension going in, getting irritated more easily, coming home drained, and then not really getting myself to basketball or sketching like I used to. So… yeah, about four months from when it started to now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or hopeless even when things at work are going fine or when you’re not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always separate it out that well in the moment.\n\nI don’t think I have many days where everything at work is fine and I’m not thinking about it at all, and then I’m just suddenly hopeless for no reason. I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “I’m not good enough,” but… yeah, I guess there are moments where I feel like I’m failing at things I should be able to handle.

It’s usually tied to situations where I’m trying to keep everything together and it still feels like I’m dropping pieces of it. Then I start going over it in my head, like, “Why couldn’t I just stay on top of that?” or “Other people seem to manage this without getting so wound up.” I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking I’m a failure as a person, but I do get frustrated with myself.

And maybe that’s part of what bothers me — I know I’m trying. It’s not like I’m slacking or I don’t care. So when things still don’t go well, it hits harder, because then I’m left wondering what else I’m supposed to do. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean, but that’s how it feels.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can point to many days like that, honestly. Like, if you mean a day where nothing at work is bothering me, nothing else is going on, and I still just wake up feeling low for no clear reason… I haven’t really noticed that as its own separate thing.

Sometimes I’ll feel kind of flat or down on a quieter day, but when I think about it, it still feels like it’s connected to the whole situation somehow — like I’m carrying over stress from before, or I’m already thinking ahead to what’s waiting for me. It’s hard to completely separate it out, because even when nothing is actively happening right that second, it’s still sort of in the background.

So… I guess my answer is no, not really, or at least not that I can clearly identify. It doesn’t feel like it just comes out of nowhere.

I’ll be at home, but I’m thinking about whether I missed something in SAP, or whether a cycle count got pushed again, or whether I snapped at somebody over a small error and made things worse. So even outside work, I can still feel on edge if my mind goes back to that role.\n\nBut if I’m truly not thinking about the warehouse or the lead responsibilities, it’s not as intense. The tension is really tied to that whole situation — walking in already braced for something to go wrong, getting irritated over little things I used to handle, then coming home drained and not even wanting to play basketball or work on my sketches. It’s like I don’t fully shut it off once I leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure or interest in almost anything, not just basketball or sketching, but other things you used to enjoy as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s almost anything, no. I mean, basketball and sketching are the big ones I notice because those were kind of my regular outlets, so when I stop doing those it stands out.\n\nWith other things… I guess it’s harder for me to tell. But if you mean, like, no pleasure in anything at all, I don’t think that’s really what’s going on. If something takes initiative, like deciding to go play or sitting down and starting a drawing, that’s where I’ve been getting stuck. It’s more that I’m worn down and kind of preoccupied, and that makes the things I used to enjoy feel harder to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel any interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’m completely incapable of enjoying anything if work isn’t on my mind, but it has gotten harder. That’s probably why I’m here, honestly. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.\n\nI walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. Same with sketching. I used to just sit down and start messing around with a drawing, and now I’ll have the thought, but I don’t follow through. It’s like the interest is still somewhere in there, but I don’t have the energy to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to feel any interest or pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, even when your mind is completely off work and the stress of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think so, but I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I can separate it cleanly in my head. Even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, the warehouse,” or replaying something that happened, it feels like the stress has already kind of taken up space in me.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing before anything even happens. Then it’ll be some small SAP error or someone missing a detail, and I’ll snap more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, which just makes me feel worse about the whole thing.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if my mind is technically off work, I’m just drained. Lately I’ll think about doing those things, but I don’t really feel that same pull, or I feel too worn out to start. It’s not that I hate them now or that they never sound good, but the interest and pleasure are harder to get to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve felt little interest or pleasure in doing things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or the stress from the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve noticed it, though I keep feeling like I need to explain it because it’s not like I just woke up one day and stopped liking things.\n\nIt’s more that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’m carrying all this tension around. I’ll get to the warehouse already on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then some little SAP error comes up or someone misses a small detail, and I react way bigger than I should. Then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that just makes me feel like I’m falling behind at something I used to handle fine.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if I’m not sitting there actively thinking about work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be something I’d do without thinking too hard about it, and sketching was the same — I could just sit down and mess around with it. Lately, I’ll think, “I should go play,” or “I should work on a sketch,” but I don’t really get myself there. It feels like the enjoyment is still there somewhere, but I’m too worn out to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the tension, irritability at work, losing interest in basketball and sketching, and feeling drained — from the time these symptoms started until now?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now, I’d say. It started around when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead role.\n\nI don’t remember the exact day where I was like, “Okay, this is different,” but that’s the turning point in my mind. Before that, work had stressful days, sure, but it didn’t follow me around the same way. Since that change, it’s just been this steady thing — the tension going in, getting irritated more easily, coming home drained, and then not really getting myself to basketball or sketching like I used to. So… yeah, about four months from when it started to now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or hopeless even when things at work are going fine or when you’re not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always separate it out that well in the moment.\n\nI don’t think I have many days where everything at work is fine and I’m not thinking about it at all, and then I’m just suddenly hopeless for no reason. It’s more like… even on quieter days, there’s still this leftover heaviness or worry in the background, if that makes sense. So I might feel down, but I usually connect it somehow to how things have been going overall, or to feeling worn out from it.\n\nIf you mean, like, completely independent of work — where I’m having a good day there, not stressed about it, and still feel hopeless anyway — I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing. It’s hard to say, but it feels tied to everything that’s been happening more than just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re failing at things even when you’re trying your best?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “I’m not good enough,” but… yeah, I guess there are moments where I feel like I’m failing at things I should be able to handle.\n\nIt’s usually tied to situations where I’m trying to keep everything together and it still feels like I’m dropping pieces of it. It’s more that I’m worn down and kind of preoccupied, and that makes the things I used to enjoy feel harder to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel any interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’m completely incapable of enjoying anything if work isn’t on my mind, but it has gotten harder. That’s probably why I’m here, honestly. Ever since they put me in that acting workflow lead role, I feel like it changed how I move through the whole day.\n\nI walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then it’ll be some little SAP error or a small mistake someone makes, and I catch myself snapping over it more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, so that piles on too. By the time I get home, even if I’m technically not thinking about work right that second, I’m just… drained.\n\nSo with basketball, I’ll think, “I should go play, that would probably help,” but getting myself to actually do it feels like a lot. Same with sketching. I used to just sit down and start messing around with a drawing, and now I’ll have the thought, but I don’t follow through. It’s like the interest is still somewhere in there, but I don’t have the energy to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to feel any interest or pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, even when your mind is completely off work and the stress of the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think so, but I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I can separate it cleanly in my head. Even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, the warehouse,” or replaying something that happened, it feels like the stress has already kind of taken up space in me.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing before anything even happens. Then it’ll be some small SAP error or someone missing a detail, and I’ll snap more than I should. And then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, which just makes me feel worse about the whole thing.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if my mind is technically off work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be automatic for me — like, I’d go play and it would clear my head. And sketching was the same kind of thing, something I could just sit down and do. Lately I’ll think about doing those things, but I don’t really feel that same pull, or I feel too worn out to start. It’s not that I hate them now or that they never sound good, but the interest and pleasure are harder to get to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve felt little interest or pleasure in doing things you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or the stress from the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve noticed it, though I keep feeling like I need to explain it because it’s not like I just woke up one day and stopped liking things.\n\nIt’s more that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’m carrying all this tension around. I’ll get to the warehouse already on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong, and then some little SAP error comes up or someone misses a small detail, and I react way bigger than I should. Then I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that just makes me feel like I’m falling behind at something I used to handle fine.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if I’m not sitting there actively thinking about work, I’m just drained. Basketball used to be something I’d do without thinking too hard about it, and sketching was the same — I could just sit down and mess around with it. Lately, I’ll think, “I should go play,” or “I should work on a sketch,” but I don’t really get myself there. It feels like the enjoyment is still there somewhere, but I’m too worn out to reach it most days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the tension, irritability at work, losing interest in basketball and sketching, and feeling drained — from the time these symptoms started until now?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now, I’d say. It started around when they merged the inventory teams and put me in that acting workflow lead role.\n\nI don’t remember the exact day where I was like, “Okay, this is different,” but that’s the turning point in my mind. Before that, work had stressful days, sure, but it didn’t follow me around the same way. Since that change, it’s just been this steady thing — the tension going in, getting irritated more easily, coming home drained, and then not really getting myself to basketball or sketching like I used to. So… yeah, about four months from when it started to now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or hopeless even when things at work are going fine or when you’re not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always separate it out that well in the moment.\n\nI don’t think I have many days where everything at work is fine and I’m not thinking about it at all, and then I’m just suddenly hopeless for no reason. It’s hard to say, but it feels tied to everything that’s been happening more than just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re failing at things even when you’re trying your best?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “I’m not good enough,” but… yeah, I guess there are moments where I feel like I’m failing at things I should be able to handle.\n\nIt’s usually tied to situations where I’m trying to keep everything together and it still feels like I’m dropping pieces of it. Then I start going over it in my head, like, “Why couldn’t I just stay on top of that?” or “Other people seem to manage this without getting so wound up.” I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking I’m a failure as a person, but I do get frustrated with myself.\n\nAnd maybe that’s part of what bothers me — I know I’m trying. It’s not like I’m slacking or I don’t care. So when things still don’t go well, it hits harder, because then I’m left wondering what else I’m supposed to do. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days when you felt down or low even when nothing particularly stressful or upsetting was happening at work or in your life at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to many days like that, honestly. It’s hard to completely separate it out, because even when nothing is actively happening right that second, it’s still sort of in the background.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not really, or at least not that I can clearly identify. It doesn’t feel like it just comes out of nowhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say all activities, no. That feels too broad for what I’ve noticed.

There are definitely things I don’t reach for as much, or I’ll start doing something and feel like I’m not really getting the same enjoyment out of it in the moment. But if you’re asking whether everything outside of work has gone completely flat — like food, watching something, talking to people, being outside, little errands, whatever — I don’t think that’s true.

It’s more inconsistent than that. Sometimes I still can enjoy things, especially if I can actually get myself into them and stop mentally circling back to everything else. Other times I’m just kind of distracted or tired and it takes the fun out of it. So I wouldn’t describe it as losing interest or pleasure in all activities. It’s more like my enjoyment is harder to access sometimes, if that makes sense.

But lately I just don’t show up.
